# Bongbong Marcos

"Bongbong" redirects here. For other uses, see [Bongbong (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongbong_\(disambiguation\)).

In this [Filipino name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_name), the [middle name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_name) or maternal  [family name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname) is *Romualdez* and the surname or paternal family name is *Marcos*.

**Ferdinand** "**Bongbong**" **Romualdez Marcos Jr.** (born September 13, 1957), commonly referred to by the initials **BBM** or **PBBM**, is a Filipino politician who has served as the 17th [president of the Philippines](271126.md) since 2022.[^cite_note-1][^cite_note-2] He is the second child and only son of 10th president [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) and former first lady [Imelda Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imelda_Marcos).[^cite_note-3]

In 1980, Marcos was elected vice governor of [Ilocos Norte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte), running unopposed with the [Kilusang Bagong Lipunan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilusang_Bagong_Lipunan) party of his father, who [was ruling the Philippines under martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_under_Ferdinand_Marcos) at the time.[^cite_note-Ellison2005-4] He then became [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Ilocos_Norte) in 1983, holding that office until his family was ousted from power by the [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution) and fled into exile in Hawaii in February 1986. After the death of his father in 1989, President [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino) allowed his family to return to the Philippines to face various charges; in October 1991, he became the first Marcos to return since the revolution. Marcos and his mother, Imelda, are currently facing arrest in the United States for defying a court order to pay [US\$](18717338.md)353 million ([₱](227672.md)17,385,250,000 in 2025) in restitution to [human rights abuse victims during his father's dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_of_the_Marcos_dictatorship).[^cite_note-bongbongCantVisitUSA-5] As president, however, he may enter the United States by virtue of diplomatic immunity.[^cite_note-6]

Marcos was elected as the [representative](293605.md) of [Ilocos Norte's second district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte's_2nd_congressional_district), serving from 1992 to 1995. He was elected governor again in 1998. After nine years, he returned to his previous position as representative from 2007 to 2010, before entering the [Senate of the Philippines](299521.md) under the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party) for a single term from 2010 to 2016.[^cite_note-7] Marcos unsuccessfully ran for [vice president](2271639.md) in the [2016 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_general_election), narrowly losing to [Camarines Sur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camarines_Sur's_3rd_congressional_district) representative [Leni Robredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Robredo). Marcos contested the result at the [Presidential Electoral Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Electoral_Tribunal) but his electoral protest was unanimously dismissed in 2021 after the pilot recount resulted in Robredo widening her lead by 15,093 additional votes.

Marcos ran for president in the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_presidential_election) under the [Partido Federal ng Pilipinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partido_Federal_ng_Pilipinas),[^cite_note-8] which he won by a landslide with nearly 59% of the vote.[^cite_note-Verizon-2022-9][^cite_note-Morales-2022-10] During his campaign, he refrained from participating in presidential debates. Although President [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md) had expressed criticisms of Marcos as a "weak leader", Duterte's daughter [Sara](42504861.md) ran as Marcos' running mate and won a majority of the votes, with their [UniTeam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UniTeam) coalition being credited for their resounding electoral success. His win was the largest since [1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Philippine_presidential_election_and_referendum), when his father won 88% of the votes due to a boycott by the opposition.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13] His presidential campaign received criticism from fact-checkers and disinformation scholars for being driven by historical negationism aimed at [rehabilitating the Marcos brand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_distortion_regarding_Ferdinand_Marcos) and smearing his rivals.[^cite_note-filipinosFallFakeHistory-14]

As president, Marcos concurrently served as the [Secretary of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Agriculture_\(Philippines\)) in his initial 16 months. He continued the [Build! Build! Build!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build!_Build!_Build!) infrastructure program of President Duterte through his own program called "[Build Better More](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Better_More)". Foreign policy has been characterized by efforts to reestablish closer ties with the [United States](3434750.md), an abrupt shift from the China-oriented policies of the Duterte administration. Amidst China's increased [territorial encroachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_reactions_to_China's_maritime_activities_in_the_South_China_Sea#The_Philippines) in the maritime area within the [West Philippine Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philippine_Sea) that has led to standoffs with Filipino vessels, Marcos has repeatedly affirmed Philippine jurisdiction over the area, stating in 2023 that "This country will not lose an inch of its territory".[^cite_note-15] In 2024, Marcos banned [Philippine offshore gaming operators](61693885.md) that had proliferated under the previous administration, while Vice President [Sara Duterte](42504861.md) resigned from his cabinet amidst a growing rift in the [Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_family) and [Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duterte_family) political families. The year 2025 was marked by [controversy over the national budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Appropriations_Act_of_2025), the [impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Sara_Duterte) of Vice President Duterte, the [arrest](79430656.md) of former president Rodrigo Duterte, and a [massive infrastructure corruption scandal](80804811.md) he had initially publicized and denounced.[^cite_note-16]

## Early life and education

Ferdinand Romualdez Marcos Jr. was born on September 13, 1957 to [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) and [Imelda Romualdez Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imelda_Marcos), at a hospital in [Santa Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa), Manila, Philippines, which became known as the [Our Lady of Lourdes Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Lourdes_Hospital_\(Manila\)) the following year.[^cite_note-19] At the time of his birth, his father Ferdinand was the representative for the [second district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte's_2nd_congressional_district) of [Ilocos Norte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte), and became a senator two years later. His godfathers include the "[Marcos cronies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_cronies)" [Eduardo "Danding" Cojuangco Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danding_Cojuangco)[^cite_note-Seagrave1988-20]<sup>: 286 </sup> and [Jose Yao Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Yao_Campos).[^cite_note-FloresWilsonLee20060508-21]

### Education

Marcos first studied at the [Institución Teresiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Pedro_Poveda_College) in [Quezon City](284223.md) and [La Salle Green Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Salle_Green_Hills) in [Mandaluyong](359686.md), where he obtained his kindergarten and elementary education, respectively.[^cite_note-SenateGovPH-Bongbong-22][^cite_note-23]

In 1970, Marcos was sent to England where he lived and studied at [Worth School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worth_School), an all-boys [Benedictine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedictine) institution in [West Sussex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Sussex).[^cite_note-SydneyMorningHerald20121124-24][^cite_note-Legaspi-25] He was studying there when his father [declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_1081) [martial law throughout the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_under_Ferdinand_Marcos) in 1972.[^cite_note-SydneyMorningHerald20121124-24][^cite_note-Legaspi-25]

Marcos attended the [Center for Research and Communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Asia_and_the_Pacific), where he took a special diploma course in economics, but did not finish.[^cite_note-Marcos_admits_not_finishing_‘special_diploma’_course_in_UA\&P-26][^cite_note-Marcos_admits_not_finishing_UA\&P_certificate_program-27] He then enrolled at [St Edmund Hall, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Edmund_Hall,_Oxford), to study [philosophy, politics and economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy,_politics_and_economics) (PPE). Despite his false claims that he graduated with a bachelor of arts in PPE,[^cite_note-28] he did not obtain such a degree.[^cite_note-rappler.com-29][^cite_note-30][^cite_note-cnnphilippines.com-31] Marcos passed philosophy, but failed economics, and failed politics twice, making him ineligible for graduation.[^cite_note-kasarinlan1-32][^cite_note-iTutoredBongbong-33] Instead, he received a special diploma in social studies,[^cite_note-cnnphilippines.com-31] which was awarded mainly to non-graduates and is currently no longer offered by the university.[^cite_note-rappler.com-29][^cite_note-documentsBongbongEducationOxford-34] Marcos still falsely claims that he obtained a degree from the University of Oxford despite the university confirming in 2015 that Marcos did not finish his degree.[^cite_note-oxfordNoDegree-35]

Marcos enrolled in the master's in [business administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Administration) program at the [Wharton School of Business, University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharton_School_of_the_University_of_Pennsylvania), in [Philadelphia](50585.md), United States, which he failed to complete. Marcos asserts that he withdrew from the program after his election to be Vice Governor of Ilocos Norte in 1980.[^cite_note-36] The [Presidential Commission on Good Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Commission_on_Good_Government) later reported that his tuition, his US\$10,000 (₱492,500 in 2025) monthly allowance, and the estate he lived in while studying at Wharton, were paid using funds that could be traced partly to the intelligence funds of the Office of the President, and partly to some of the fifteen bank accounts that the Marcoses had secretly opened in the US under assumed names.[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37]

### Early public roles

See also: [Iginuhit ng Tadhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iginuhit_ng_Tadhana) and [The Beatles' 1966 tour of Germany, Japan and the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles'_1966_tour_of_Germany,_Japan_and_the_Philippines)



Ferdinand and Imelda Marcos meeting with United States president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), shown holding Bongbong, in 1969

Marcos was thrust into the national limelight as early as when he was three years old, and the scrutiny became even more intense when his father first ran for President of the Philippines in 1965,[^cite_note-Lo20100413-38] when he was eight years old.[^cite_note-SydneyMorningHerald20121124-24][^cite_note-Legaspi-25][^cite_note-Seagrave1988-20]

During his father's 1965 campaign, Marcos played himself in the [Sampaguita Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampaguita_Pictures) film *[Iginuhit ng Tadhana (The Ferdinand E. Marcos Story)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iginuhit_ng_Tadhana_\(The_Ferdinand_E._Marcos_Story\))*, a biopic based on the novel *For Every Tear a Victory*.[^cite_note-Gomez150826-39][^cite_note-Lo20100413-38] The young Marcos was portrayed giving a speech towards the end of the film, in which he says that he would like to be a politician when he grows up.[^cite_note-Geronimo20151012-40] The public relations value of the film is credited for having helped the elder Marcos win the [1965 Philippine elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Philippine_presidential_election).[^cite_note-Garcia2016-41]

A young Bongbong Marcos and his sister Imee played a small role in the controversial "[Manila incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles'_1966_tour_of_Germany,_Japan_and_the_Philippines#Philippines)" of [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) in July 1966, just six months after their father assumed the presidency.[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42][^cite_note-43]<sup>: 200 </sup> Bongbong and Imee were among 400 children whom their mother Imelda brought to Malacañang Palace for a reception in which they expected the Beatles to show up.[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42] The four band members claimed not to know about the event, and refused to attend.[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42]

As the event went on without them, the Marcos children were interviewed. Bongbong, referring to the group's long hair, was quoted saying "I'd like to pounce on the Beatles and cut off their hair! Don't anybody dare me to do anything, because I'll do it, just to see how game the Beatles are."[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42] Imee, meantime, was quoted saying "There is only one song I like from the Beatles, and it's [Run for Your Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Run_for_Your_Life_\(Beatles_song\))."[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42]—a quote which media later associated with the way the Beatles scrambled out of Manila, receiving rough treatment at [Manila International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_International_Airport).[^cite_note-OliverXAReyes20170524-42]

Beatles lead guitarist [George Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Harrison) later accused the Marcoses of inciting Filipinos to mob the band as they tried to leave the country for not showing up at the reception, saying in a 1986 interview at [NBC's *Today Show*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Today_\(American_TV_program\)) that the Marcoses "tried to kill \[them]."[^cite_note-SalvaESQ2021-44][^cite_note-Twit-45] Harrison said that their plane was not allowed to leave Manila until their manager, [Brian Epstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Epstein), refunded the concert ticket money.[^cite_note-SalvaESQ2021-44][^cite_note-Twit-45]

The *[Manila Bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bulletin)* reported in 2015 that Marcos had once invited Beatles drummer [Ringo Starr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringo_Starr) to return to the Philippines "to bring closure" to the incident.[^cite_note-25_things-46] The incident was brought up in the media again after a 2021 interview between Marcos and actress [Toni Gonzaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toni_Gonzaga), when he was asked about which musicians he idolized, and he casually mentioned that he was friends with [Mick Jagger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mick_Jagger) of [the Rolling Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rolling_Stones) and members of the Beatles.[^cite_note-SalvaESQ2021-44]

Marcos was still a minor in 1972 [when martial law was declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_under_Ferdinand_Marcos). Marcos turned 18 in 1975[^cite_note-mlchroniclesaccountable-47][^cite_note-EricaSauler20160205-48]—a year after he graduated from Worth School.[^cite_note-49]

## Roles in the Marcos regime

### Vice governorship and governorship in Ilocos Norte

Marcos's first formal role in a political office came with his election as Vice Governor of Ilocos Norte (1980–1983) at the age of 22.  On March 23, 1983, he was installed as the [Governor of Ilocos Norte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Ilocos_Norte), replacing his aunt Elizabeth Marcos-Keon, who had resigned from the post for health reasons.[^cite_note-50] In 1983, he led a group of young Filipino leaders on a 10-day diplomatic mission to China to mark the tenth anniversary of [Philippine–Chinese relations](33734272.md).[^cite_note-51] He stayed in office until the [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution) in 1986.

During Marcos's term, at least two [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killings_and_forced_disappearances_in_the_Philippines) took place in Ilocos Norte, as documented by the Martial Law Victims Association of Ilocos Norte (MLVAIN).[^cite_note-Cornelio20160415-52][^cite_note-53]

### Chairmanship of PHILCOMSAT Board

Marcos was appointed by his father to be chairman of the board of the [Philippine Communications Satellite Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Communications_Satellite_Corporation) (PHILCOMSAT) in early 1985.[^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54] In a prominent example of what Finance Minister [Jaime Ongpin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Ongpin) later branded "[crony capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crony_capitalism)", the Marcos administration had sold its majority shares to [Marcos cronies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_cronies) such as [Roberto S. Benedicto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_S._Benedicto),[^cite_note-Scott1986-55] [Manuel H. Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cronies_of_Ferdinand_Marcos),[^cite_note-Scott1986-55] [Jose Yao Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Yao_Campos),[^cite_note-Butterfield19860330-56] and [Rolando Gapud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolando_Gapud)[^cite_note-Butterfield19860330-56] in 1982, despite being very profitable because of its role as the sole agent for the Philippines' link to global satellite network Intelsat.[^cite_note-Scott1986-55]

President Marcos acquired a 39.9% share in the company, through front companies under Campos and Gapud.[^cite_note-Butterfield19860330-56] This allowed President Marcos to appoint his son as the chairman of the Philcomsat board in early 1985, allowing the young Marcos to draw a monthly salary "ranging from US\$9,700 to US\$97,000"[^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54][^cite_note-Scott1986-55] (₱477,725 to ₱4,777,250 in 2025) despite rarely visiting the office and having no duties there.[^cite_note-Scott1986-55][^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54] PHILCOMSAT was one of five telecommunications firms sequestered by the Philippine government in 1986.[^cite_note-Scott1986-55]

### Ill-gotten Marcos family wealth

Main article: [Unexplained wealth of the Marcos family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unexplained_wealth_of_the_Marcos_family)

After the Marcos family went into exile in 1986, the [Presidential Commission on Good Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Commission_on_Good_Government) found that the three Marcos children benefited significantly[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37][^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54][^cite_note-Garcia2106-57] from what the [Supreme Court of the Philippines](1335534.md) defined as "ill-gotten wealth" of the Marcos family.[^cite_note-scImeldaMarcosvsRP-58][^cite_note-TiongsonMayrina-59][^cite_note-Salonga2000-60]

Aside from the tuition, US\$10,000 (₱492,500 in 2025) monthly allowance, and the estates used by Marcos Jr. and Imee Marcos during their respective studies at Wharton and Princeton,[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37] each of the Marcos children was assigned a [mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_mansions) in the Metro Manila area, as well as in [Baguio](162480.md), the Philippines' designated summer capital.[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37] Properties specifically said to have been given to Marcos Jr. included the Wigwam House compound on Outlook Drive in Baguio[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37] and the Seaside Mansion Compound in [Parañaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%B1aque).[^cite_note-Manapat2020-37]

In addition, by the time their father was ousted from power in 1986, both Marcos Jr. and Imee held key posts in the Marcos administration.[^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54] Imee was already 30 when she was appointed as the national head of the Kabataang Barangay in the late 1970s,[^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54] and Marcos Jr. was in his 20s when he took up the vice-gubernatorial post for the province of Ilocos Norte in 1980, and then became governor of that province from 1983 until the Marcos family was ousted from Malacañang in 1986.[^cite_note-JamainePunzalan20161125-54]

### As an Officer of the Philippine Constabulary

Marcos Jr. was commissioned as a Second Lieutenant in the [Philippine Constabulary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Constabulary) Reserve in 1978, as a member of the Presidential Security Command, while serving as a special presidential assistant for his father. He had also undergone a six-month officer course at the Philippine Marine Training Center.[^cite_note-61]

## EDSA revolution and exile (1986–1991)

Further information: [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution)

During the last days of the 1986 [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution), Bongbong Marcos, dressed in combat fatigues to project his warlike stance,[^cite_note-usSet5Conditions-62] pushed his father [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) to give the order to his remaining troops to attack and blow up [Camp Crame](1697214.md) despite the presence of hundreds of thousands of civilians there. The elder Marcos did not follow his son's urgings.[^cite_note-fmLastDayPalace-63]

Fearful of a scenario in which Marcos's presence in the Philippines would lead to a civil war,[^cite_note-StuartSantiago1995-64] the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_administration) withdrew its support for the Marcos government, and flew Marcos and a party of about 80 individuals[^cite_note-DavidHolley19860228-65] – the extended Marcos family and a number of close associates[^cite_note-APmanifest19860311-66] – from the Philippines to Hawaii despite Ferdinand Marcos's objections.[^cite_note-StuartSantiago1995-64] Bongbong Marcos and his family were on the flight with his parents.[^cite_note-DavidHolley19860227-67][^cite_note-68]

Soon after arriving in Hawaii, the younger Marcos participated in an attempt to withdraw US\$200 million (₱9,849,999,999.96 in 2025) from a [secret family bank account](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_secrecy) with [Credit Suisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_Suisse) in Switzerland,[^cite_note-SCMP2011-69] an act which eventually led to the Swiss government freezing the Marcoses' bank accounts in late March that year.[^cite_note-WaPoSwissFreeze-70]

The Marcoses initially stayed at [Hickam Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hickam_Air_Force_Base) at the expense of the U.S. government. A month after arriving in [Honolulu](13887.md), they moved into a pair of residences in [Makiki Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makiki_Heights), Honolulu, which were registered to [Marcos cronies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_cronies) [Antonio Floirendo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Floirendo) and Bienvenido and Gliceria Tantoco.[^cite_note-DavidHolley19860228-65]

Ferdinand Marcos eventually died in exile three years later, in 1989,[^cite_note-Richburg\&Branigin19890929-71] with Marcos Jr. being the only family member present at his father's deathbed.[^cite_note-Aruiza1991-72]

## Return to the Philippines; political career (1991–2022)

In the early 1990s, President [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino) permitted the return of the remaining members of the Marcos family to the Philippines to face various charges.[^cite_note-SethMydans19911104-73] Bongbong Marcos flew on a private plane from Singapore to the Philippines and landed in [Laoag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laoag), [Ilocos Norte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte) on October 31, 1991, becoming the first Marcos family member to return to the Philippines since 1986; his mother Imelda followed suit four days later.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] He soon sought political office, beginning in the family's traditional [fiefdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiefdom) in Ilocos Norte.[^cite_note-77]

### House of Representatives, first term

Further information: [1992 Philippine House of Representatives elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Philippine_House_of_Representatives_elections)

Marcos ran for and was elected representative of the [second district of Ilocos Norte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Norte's_2nd_congressional_district) to the [Philippine House of Representatives](293605.md) (1992–1995).[^cite_note-78] When his mother, Imelda Marcos, ran for president in the same election, he decided against supporting her candidacy, and instead expressed support for his godfather Danding Cojuangco.[^cite_note-79] During his term, Marcos was the author of 29 House bills and co-author of 90 more, which includes those that paved the way for the creation of the [Department of Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Energy_\(Philippines\)) and the [National Youth Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Youth_Commission_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80] He also allocated most of his Countryside Development Fund (CDF) to organizing the cooperatives of teachers and farmers in his home province.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-About_Bongbong_Marcos-82] In October 1992, he led a group of ten representatives in attending the first sports summit in the Philippines, held in [Baguio](162480.md).[^cite_note-83] In late 1994, he was made president of the [Kilusang Bagong Lipunan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilusang_Bagong_Lipunan) party, which is known for its support for the Marcos regime.[^cite_note-84]

[In 1995](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Philippine_Senate_election), Marcos ran for the Senate under the [NPC-led coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_People's_Coalition) but lost, placing only 16th.[^cite_note-85]

#### Compromise deal attempt

In 1995, Bongbong Marcos pushed a deal to allow the Marcos family to keep a quarter of the estimated US\$2 billion to US\$10 billion (₱98,499,999,999.61 to ₱492,499,999,998.03 in 2025) that the Philippine government had still not recovered from them, on the condition that all civil cases be dropped – a deal that was eventually struck down by the Philippines' Supreme Court.[^cite_note-SCMP2011-69]

### Ilocos Norte governor, second stint

Having previously served as Ilocos Norte governor from 1983 to 1986, Marcos was again elected as governor of Ilocos Norte in 1998, running against his father's closest friend and ally, [Roque Ablan Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roque_Ablan_Jr.) He served for three consecutive terms ending in 2007.[^cite_note-86]

### House of Representatives, second term

In 2007, Marcos ran unopposed for the congressional seat previously held by his older sister [Imee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imee_Marcos).[^cite_note-87] He was then appointed as deputy minority leader of the House of Representatives. During this term, Marcos supported the passage of the Philippine Archipelagic Baselines Law, or Republic Act No. 9522.[^cite_note-88] He also wrote his own version of the law, but the bill only remained in the House Committee on Foreign Affairs.[^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80][^cite_note-89] He also promoted the Republic Act No. 9502 (Universally Accessible Cheaper and Quality Medicines Act) which was enacted on 2009.[^cite_note-90]

### Senate career



Senator Marcos during a forum in June 2014



Portrait during his stint as senator

Further information: [2010 Philippine Senate election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Philippine_Senate_election)

Marcos made a second attempt for the Senate in [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Philippine_general_election). On November 20, 2009, the KBL forged an alliance with the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party) (NP) between Marcos and NP chair Senator [Manny Villar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Villar) at the [Laurel House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_P._Laurel_Residence) in [Mandaluyong](359686.md). Marcos became a guest senatorial candidate of the NP through this alliance.[^cite_note-Philstar_NP_KBL-91] Marcos was later removed as a member by the KBL National Executive Committee on November 23, 2009.[^cite_note-92] As such, the NP broke its alliance with the KBL due to internal conflicts within the party, however Marcos remained part of the NP senatorial lineup.[^cite_note-Philstar_NP_KBL-91] He was proclaimed as one of the winning senatorial candidates of the [2010 senate elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Philippine_Senate_election). He took office on June 30, 2010.

In the [15th Congress (2010–2013)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_Congress_of_the_Philippines), Marcos authored 34 Senate bills. He also co-authored 17 bills of which seven were enacted into law[^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80] – most notably the Anti-Drunk and Drugged Driving Act whose principal author was Senator [Vicente Sotto III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Sotto_III); the [Cybercrime Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cybercrime_Prevention_Act_of_2012) whose principal author was Senator [Edgardo Angara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgardo_Angara); and the Expanded Anti-Trafficking in Persons and the National Health Insurance Acts, both of which were principally authored by Senator [Loren Legarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loren_Legarda).

Marcos delivering a sponsorship speech on the charter of the City of [General Trias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Trias) in 2015.

In the [16th Congress (2013–2016)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Congress_of_the_Philippines), Marcos filed 52 bills, of which 28 were refiled from the 15th Congress. One of them was enacted into law: Senate Bill No. 1186, which sought the postponement of the [2013 Sangguniang Kabataan (SK) elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Philippine_barangay_elections), was enacted as Republic Act No. 10632 on October 3, 2013.[^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80]

Marcos also co-authored 4 Senate bills in the 16th Congress. One of them, Senate Bill No. 712 which was principally authored by [Ralph Recto](7787924.md), was enacted as Republic Act No. 10645, the Expanded Senior Citizens Act of 2010.[^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80][^cite_note-93]

He was the chair of the Senate committees on urban planning, housing and resettlement, local government, and public works.[^cite_note-94] He also chaired the oversight committee on the [Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao (ARMM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Region_in_Muslim_Mindanao) Organic Act, the congressional oversight panel on the Special Purpose Vehicle Act, and a select oversight committee on [barangay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barangay) affairs.[^cite_note-SenateGovPH-Bongbong-22][^cite_note-Bueza-2015-80]

#### 2014 pork barrel scam

In 2014, Bongbong Marcos was implicated by [Janet Lim Napoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Lim_Napoles)[^cite_note-20senatorsPDAF-95] and Benhur Luy[^cite_note-25Senators-96] in the [Priority Development Assistance Fund (PDAF) Pork Barrel scam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priority_Development_Assistance_Fund_scam) through agent Catherine Mae "Maya" Santos.[^cite_note-affidavitNapoles-97] He allegedly channeled ₱100 million through 4 fake [NGOs](46539.md) linked with Napoles.[^cite_note-abadShouldHaveChecked-98] Marcos claimed that the large amounts of money was released by the budget department without his knowledge and that his signatures were forged.[^cite_note-MarcosFundMisuse-99] In connection to the PDAF scam, Marcos was also sued for plunder by iBalik ang Bilyones ng Mamamayan (iBBM), an alliance of youth organizations. The group cited Luy's digital files, which showed bogus NGOs with shady or non-existent offices.[^cite_note-bongbongMarcosPlunder-100]

#### 2016 Commission on Audit suit

In 2016, Marcos was also sued for *plunder* for funneling ₱205 million of his PDAF via 9 special allotment release orders (SARO) to the following bogus foundations from October 2011 to January 2013, according to Luy's digital files:[^cite_note-bongbongMarcosPlunder-100]

- Social Development Program for Farmers Foundation (SDPFFI) – ₱15 million

- Countrywide Agri and Rural Economic Development Foundation (CARED) – ₱35 million

- People's Organization for Progress and Development Foundation (POPDFI) – ₱40 million

- Health Education Assistance Resettlement Training Services (HEARTS) – ₱10 million

- Kaupdanan Para Sa Mangunguma Foundation (KMFI) – ₱20 million

- National Livelihood Development Corporation (NLDC) – ₱100 million

These NGOs were found by the [Commission on Audit (COA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Audit_of_the_Philippines) as bogus with shady or non-existent offices.[^cite_note-bongbongMarcosPlunder-100]

### 2016 vice presidential campaign

Further information: [2016 Philippine presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election)

Main article: [Miriam Defensor Santiago 2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miriam_Defensor_Santiago_2016_presidential_campaign)



Wikiquote has quotations related to ***[Ferdinand Marcos Jr.'s 2016 Vice Presidential campaign](https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos_Jr.)***.

On October 5, 2015, Marcos announced via his website that he would run for [Vice President of the Philippines](2271639.md) in the [2016 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_general_election), stating "I have decided to run for vice president in the May 2016 elections."[^cite_note-CNN_Philippines_2015-10-05-101][^cite_note-102] Marcos ran as an independent candidate.[^cite_note-103] Prior to his announcement, he had declined an invitation by presidential candidate, Vice President [Jejomar Binay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jejomar_Binay), to become his running mate.[^cite_note-104] On October 15, 2015, presidential candidate [Miriam Defensor Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miriam_Defensor_Santiago) confirmed that Marcos would serve as her running mate.[^cite_note-105]

Marcos placed second in the tightly contested vice presidential race, losing to [Camarines Sur's 3rd district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camarines_Sur's_3rd_congressional_district) representative [Leni Robredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Robredo), who won by a margin of 263,473 votes,[^cite_note-slim_margin_1-106][^cite_note-slim_margin_2-107] one of the closest since [Fernando Lopez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Lopez)'s victory in the 1965 vice presidential election.

#### Election results protest

Main article: [2016 Philippine presidential election § Electoral protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election#Electoral_protest)

Marcos challenged the results of the election, lodging an electoral protest against Leni Robredo on June 29, 2016, the day before Robredo's oathtaking.[^cite_note-VeraFilesPETTimeline-108][^cite_note-109] President [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md) has stated several times that he would resign if Marcos would be his successor instead of Vice President Leni Robredo.[^cite_note-BernadetteNicolas20180816-110]

A recount began in April 2018, covering polling precincts in Iloilo and Camarines Sur, which were areas handpicked by Marcos's camp. In October 2019, the tribunal found that Robredo's lead grew by around 15,000 votes – a total of 278,566 votes from Robredo's original lead of 263,473 votes – after a recount of ballots from the 5,415 clustered precincts in Marcos's identified pilot provinces.[^cite_note-111] On February 16, 2021, the [Presidential Electoral Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Electoral_Tribunal) (PET) unanimously dismissed Bongbong Marcos's electoral protest against Leni Robredo.[^cite_note-PETdismissalSCMP-112][^cite_note-PETdismissalCNN-113][^cite_note-PETdecisionABSCBN-114][^cite_note-TetchTorresTupasPETDecision-115]

### 2022 presidential campaign and election



Marcos (center) and his running mate [Sara Duterte](42504861.md) during a grand caravan in [Quezon City](284223.md) in December 2021

Further information: [2022 Philippine presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_presidential_election)

Main article: [Bongbong Marcos 2022 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongbong_Marcos_2022_presidential_campaign)

Marcos officially launched his campaign for [president of the Philippines](271126.md) on October 5, 2021, through a video post on Facebook and YouTube.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] An interview with his wife Liza Marcos revealed that he decided to run for president while watching the film *[Ant-Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant-Man_\(film\))*,[^cite_note-cnnAntmanPresidentialRun-118][^cite_note-inquirerAntmanPresidentialRun-119] though Marcos admitted that he could not recall this moment.[^cite_note-120] He ran under the banner of the [Partido Federal ng Pilipinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partido_Federal_ng_Pilipinas) party, assuming chairmanship of the party on the same day,[^cite_note-CNN-121] while also being endorsed by his former party, the [Kilusang Bagong Lipunan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilusang_Bagong_Lipunan).[^cite_note-122] He was endorsed by controversial religious group [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md) (INC).[^cite_note-123]

Marcos filed his certificate of candidacy before the [Commission on Elections](1847922.md) the following day.[^cite_note-124] On November 16, Marcos announced his running mate to be [Davao City](489401.md) mayor [Sara Duterte](42504861.md), daughter of President [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md).[^cite_note-INQtandem-125] Under the campaign theme of unity, Marcos and Duterte's alliance was given the name "[UniTeam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UniTeam)".[^cite_note-INQtandem-125]

Seven petitions were filed against Marcos's presidential bid.[^cite_note-CNNPH-Petitions-126][^cite_note-127] Three petitions aimed to cancel his certificate of candidacy (COC), one petition aimed to declare Marcos a [nuisance candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuisance_candidate), and three petitions aimed to disqualify him. Most of the petitions were based on Marcos's [1995 conviction for failing to file tax returns](#1995_tax_case_conviction). Three disqualification petitions were consolidated and raffled to the commission's first division, while three other petitions were handed to the second division.[^cite_note-CNNPH-Petitions-126][^cite_note-Bordey-2021-128]

The final petition was handed to the first division. Marcos dismissed the petitions as nuisance petitions with no legal basis and propaganda against him.[^cite_note-129] On May 16 and 18, 2022, respectively, [two of the petitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_petitions_to_nullify_Bongbong_Marcos'_candidacy_in_the_2022_Philippine_presidential_election) were filed at the Supreme Court.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] The consolidated petitions were dismissed by the Court on June 28.[^cite_note-132]



Marcos won in 64 out of 81 provinces in the [2022 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_presidential_election).

[^cite_note-133]

Marcos regularly maintained a wide lead in presidential surveys throughout the months leading up to the May 2022 election;[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] he was the first presidential candidate in the country to attain poll ratings of over 50% from surveys conducted by [Pulse Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse_Asia) since it began polling in 1999.[^cite_note-136] His refrainment from attending all but one of the presidential debates during the campaign season was widely criticized.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

In a joint session of the [18th Congress of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_Congress_of_the_Philippines), overseen by Senate President [Tito Sotto](3873709.md) and [House Speaker](7835723.md) [Lord Allan Velasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Allan_Velasco) and stated by Senate Majority Leader [Migz Zubiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migz_Zubiri) and Majority Floor Leader [Martin Romualdez](45505193.md), Marcos was proclaimed the [president-elect of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President-elect_of_the_Philippines) on May 25, 2022, alongside his running-mate, Vice-President-elect [Sara Duterte](42504861.md). Marcos received 31,629,783 votes, or 58.77% of the total votes cast, about 16.5 million votes ahead of his closest rival, Vice President [Leni Robredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Robredo), who received over 15 million votes.[^cite_note-141]

He became the first presidential candidate to be elected by a majority since the establishment of the Fifth Republic in [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Philippine_presidential_election).[^cite_note-Verizon-2022-9][^cite_note-Morales-2022-10][^cite_note-142] According to analysts, Marcos, together with Sara Duterte, "inherited" Rodrigo Duterte's popularity when they both won landslides in the election.[^cite_note-143] Historians noted the significance of his victory as a "full circle" of the Philippines from the [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution), which deposed [his father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) from the presidency, thus marking the [Marcos family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_family)'s return to national power after 36 years.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-WaPo-146] Marcos's presidential campaign received criticism from fact-checkers and disinformation scholars, who found his campaign to be driven by historical negationism aimed at [rehabilitating the Marcos brand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_distortion_regarding_Ferdinand_Marcos) and smearing his rivals.[^cite_note-filipinosFallFakeHistory-14] His campaign has also been accused of [whitewashing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewashing_\(censorship\)) the human rights abuses and [plunder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unexplained_wealth_of_the_Marcos_family), estimated at 5 to 13 billion dollars, that took place during his father's presidency.[^cite_note-filipinosFallFakeHistory-14] *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* has noted how the historical distortionism of the Marcoses has been underway since the 2000s, while *[The New York Times](30680.md)* cited his convictions of tax fraud, including his refusal to pay his family's estate taxes, and misrepresentation of his education at the [University of Oxford](31797.md).[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150]

His majority was the largest since [1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Philippine_presidential_election_and_referendum) (surpassing his father's 18,309,360 votes); as the opposition boycotted that election, it is the largest majority since [1969](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Philippine_presidential_election) for a competitive election, and his 31-percentage point margin over his nearest opponent was the greatest since [Ramon Magsaysay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay) scored a 38-point margin over incumbent President [Elpidio Quirino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elpidio_Quirino) in [1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Philippine_presidential_election). His vote count was not only the largest ever recorded in a presidential election, but close to the sum total of the two previous records combined.

On June 20, 2022, Marcos announced that he will serve as the [Secretary of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Agriculture_\(Philippines\)) in concurrent capacity.[^cite_note-151]

In the 2022 elections, according to their statements of campaign contributions, Bongbong Marcos and Sara Duterte received tens of millions of pesos in campaign contributions from contractors, despite an election ban on taking donations from firms that do business with the Philippine government.[^cite_note-\:02-152]

## Presidency (2022–present)

Main article: [Presidency of Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bongbong_Marcos)

See also: [Presidential transition of Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Bongbong_Marcos), [Inauguration of Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Bongbong_Marcos), and [Protests against Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Bongbong_Marcos)

|  |  |

Official portrait, 2022



Marcos delivering his inaugural address



Marcos delivering [his first State of the Nation Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_State_of_the_Nation_Address_\(Philippines\)) on July 25, 2022

### Early actions

On June 30, 2022, at 12\:00 noon [PST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Standard_Time), Marcos Jr. took the oath of office as the 17th [President of the Philippines](271126.md) at the [National Museum of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_the_Philippines) and was administered the oath by Chief Justice [Alexander Gesmundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Gesmundo).[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-Marcos_officially_declared_Philippines'_next_president-154] At concurrent capacity, Marcos appointed himself as [Secretary of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Agriculture_\(Philippines\)), in order to address inflation and personally monitor the food and agricultural sectors, while enacting efforts to boost farm outputs through various loan programs, affordable pricing measures, and machinery assistance.[^cite_note-Marcos_Names_Himself_Agriculture_Chief_to_Tackle_Food_Cost-155] Marcos's first [executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_executive_orders_by_Bongbong_Marcos) as president were abolishing two offices: the [Presidential Anti Corruption Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Anti-Corruption_Commission) and the Office of the [Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Secretary_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-156]

The next day after his inauguration, Marcos signed a memorandum seeking to provide free train rides to students, and extends the free rides of the [EDSA Carousel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EDSA_Carousel) until the end of December 2022.[^cite_note-DOTr:_Marcos_extends_free_EDSA_Carousel_bus_rides,_OKs_free_train_rides_for_students-157] Twelve days later, on July 13, 2022, Marcos announced that the free train rides will only be limited to students using the [LRT Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LRT_Line_2), due to the line's access points to the [University Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Belt).[^cite_note-DOTr:_Free_train_rides_for_students_now_only_on_LRT2-158]

Two days after his inauguration, on July 2, 2022, Marcos [vetoed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vetoed) a bill sponsored by his sister Senator [Imee Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imee_Marcos) that aimed to create a [free economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_economic_zone) within [New Manila International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Manila_International_Airport). Bongbong Marcos said that the bill would cite "substantial fiscal risks", lacked coherences with existing laws, and the proposed economic zone's location near the existing [Clark Freeport and Special Economic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Freeport_and_Special_Economic_Zone); Marcos also called for further studies in establishing the planned economic zone.[^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_vetoes_bill_on_Bulacan_Airport_City_ecozone-159] On the same day, Marcos also ordered that the list of [Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantawid_Pamilyang_Pilipino_Program) beneficiaries to be cleansed after receiving reports of unqualified beneficiaries receiving cash assistance grants and downturned calls to surrender their accounts.

On July 5, 2022, five days after his inauguration, Marcos held his first cabinet meeting, which was delayed during his inauguration, and laid out his first agenda, which primarily focuses on reviving the economy in the midst of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_Philippines). During the meeting, Marcos led the discussions with his economic managers, [Finance Secretary](36182226.md) [Benjamin Diokno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Diokno), [National Economic and Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Economic_and_Development_Authority) Secretary [Arsenio Balisacan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenio_Balisacan), and [Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas](1276593.md) Governor [Felipe Medalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Medalla), to give a briefing about the country's economic status, and to lay out plans to further revive the country's economy, while combating inflation.[^cite_note-President_Marcos_focuses_on_PH_economy_in_first_Cabinet_meeting-160][^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_holds_first_Cabinet_meeting-161][^cite_note-Marcos_holds_first_Cabinet_meeting;_focus_on_PH_economy-162] Marcos also tackled issues regarding food security, transportation issues, and the reopening of face-to-face classes within the year.[^cite_note-President_Marcos_focuses_on_PH_economy_in_first_Cabinet_meeting-160] On July 23, 2022, Marcos has vetoed a bill which seeks to strengthen the Office of the Government Corporate Counsel (OGCC), as he cited that several provisions of the bill are "inequitable".[^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_vetoes_bill_strengthening_OGCC-163][^cite_note-BBM_vetoes_bill_for_govt_lawyers-164]

On July 25, 2022, the same day of his first [State of the Nation Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_State_of_the_Nation_Address_\(Philippines\)), Marcos allowed Republic Act No. 11900, known as the [Vaporized Nicotine and Non-Nicotine Products Regulation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaporized_Nicotine_and_Non-Nicotine_Products_Regulation_Act) to lapse into law. The law became controversial, due to the hounding health risks regarding the usage of [electronic cigarettes](11996885.md) and [heated tobacco products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heated_tobacco_product).[^cite_note-Controversial_bill_lowering_the_age_for_vape_access_lapses_into_law:_Palace-165] In an effort to boost the country's booster shot campaign, Marcos launched the "PinasLakas" campaign to continue administering [COVID-19 booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_booster) doses within the public, by targeting a total of at least 39 million Filipinos to get their booster shots.[^cite_note-PinasLakas_administers_3.4M_1st_booster_dose_in_100_days-166]

Two days after his first State of the Nation Address, following a meeting with Solicitor General [Menardo Guevarra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menardo_Guevarra), Presidential Legal Adviser [Juan Ponce Enrile](3322486.md), Executive Secretary [Vic Rodriguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vic_Rodriguez_\(lawyer\)), Foreign Affairs Secretary [Enrique Manalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Manalo), Justice Secretary [Jesus Crispin Remulla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Crispin_Remulla), and former presidential spokesman and lawyer [Harry Roque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Roque) on July 27, 2022, Marcos expressed that the Philippines has no intention of rejoining the [International Criminal Court](14880.md), as the death cases linked to the country's [drug war](51032591.md) of [his predecessor's administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Rodrigo_Duterte) are already being investigated by the government, and stated that the government is taking the necessary steps regarding the deaths.[^cite_note-PH_has_no_intention_of_rejoining_ICC:_Marcos-167]

On July 30, 2022, Marcos vetoed a bill which grants tax exemption on poll workers' [honoraria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honoraria) and the creation of a transport safety board, stating that the honoraria "counters the objective of the government's [Comprehensive Tax Reform Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Reform_for_Acceleration_and_Inclusion_Law)", while mentioning that the proposed creation of a transport safety board "undertakes the functions by the different agencies" within the [transport sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_vetoes_transport_safety_board,_tax-free_poll_workers’_honoraria_bills-168][^cite_note-Marcos_explains_veto_on_bill_making_tax-exempt_teachers’_poll_service_pay-169]

### Domestic policy

#### Agriculture and agrarian reforms



Marcos at a Kadiwa Project outlet in [Koronadal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koronadal), [South Cotabato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Cotabato), in June 2023

Subsequently serving as the Secretary of Agriculture, Marcos launched initiatives which aims to improve domestic agricultural output and production, while expanding measures to further establish a farm-to-market approach in providing agricultural products to local markets and far flung areas.[^cite_note-Marcos_wants_to_accelerate_efforts_to_build_agricultural_sector_back-170][^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_orders_DA:_Create_farm-to-market_road_masterplan-171] In August 2022, as [high sugar prices impacted the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_sugar_crisis) due to the effects of [Typhoon Odette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Odette) in December 2021, the [Sugar Regulatory Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Regulatory_Administration) (SRA) in August 2022 released an order to import 300,000 metric tons (660,000,000 lb) of sugar, which is aimed to reduce costs and increase the sugar stockpiles. A few days later, Marcos rejected the proposed importation,[^cite_note-172] and Malacañang deemed the move as illegal, as the move was made without Marcos's approval, nor signed by him.[^cite_note-MB-ProbeSugar-173] SRA Undersecretary Leocadio Sebastian later apologized for the move and later resigned his post on Marcos; behalf,[^cite_note-174] prompting SRA Administrator Hermenegildo Serafica and SRA board member Roland Beltran to follow suit a few days later.[^cite_note-175] The move also caused Malacañang to instigate reforms within the SRA organization,[^cite_note-176] and launched a campaign into alleged efforts of using the sugar order as a "cover measure" for [hoarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoarding) by sugar traders.[^cite_note-Marcos_urged_to_crack_down_on_sugar_hoarders-177]

In November 2022, Marcos expanded the Kadiwa Project launched by the Duterte administration, which aims to offer fresh local produces to local markets and other key areas in lower prices, and creates a direct farm-to-market approach of goods and services.[^cite_note-Kadiwa_store_program_eyed_until_February,_March_—_Bongbong_Marcos-178][^cite_note-Expanded_Kadiwa_store_project_good_for_farmers,_consumers,_agri_group_says-179] The programs is located in various areas throughout the country and temporarily occupies various facilities owned by local governments. The move is also aimed to be expanded permanently to accommodate more consumers affected by inflation.[^cite_note-Marcos_eyes_permanent_Kadiwa_stores_nationwide-180]

In January 2023, amid [rising prices of onions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932023_Philippine_onion_crisis) in the country, Marcos approved the importation of 21,060 metric tons (46,430,000 lb) of onions to cater the gap caused by low local outputs,[^cite_note-Onion_woes_'a_demand_and_supply_situation'_Marcos_says_as_prices_remain_high-181] and stated that the government was "left without a choice" despite approving the smuggled onions to be supplied in local markets.[^cite_note-Marcos_says_government_had_no_choice_but_import_onions_to_meet_demand-182]

Marcos signed his fourth executive order on September 14, 2022, which establishes a one-year [moratorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moratorium_\(law\)) on the [amortization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amortization_\(accounting\)) and [interest](146738.md) payments of [agrarian reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_reform) beneficiaries. The move is seen to assist farmers from debt payments and allows a flexible approach in financial assistance.[^cite_note-183]

In July 2023, Marcos signed the [New Agrarian Emancipation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Agrarian_Emancipation_Act), freeing at least 600,000 agrarian reform beneficiaries of decades-old debts worth ₱57-billion under the [Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Agrarian_Reform_Program).[^cite_note-184]

After serving as Secretary of Agriculture for over a year that was marked by a rise in food prices, Marcos relinquished his position and appointed [Francisco Tiu Laurel Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Tiu_Laurel_Jr.), president of a deep-sea fishing company and a donor to Marcos's 2022 presidential election campaign.[^cite_note-185]

#### Defense



Marcos with United States president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) at the [White House](33057.md), [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), in July 2025



Marcos with Japanese prime minister [Sanae Takaichi](8125532.md) at the [Naikaku Sōri Daijin Kantei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naikaku_S%C5%8Dri_Daijin_Kantei), [Tokyo](30057.md), in May 2026

In August 2022, the Marcos administration said it was considering ordering helicopters from the [United States military](32212.md), such as the [Boeing CH-47 Chinook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_CH-47_Chinook), to replace the 16 Russian [Mil Mi-17](1493374.md) military helicopters purchased by the Duterte administration, but cancelled the program a few days before the end of Duterte's term out of concerns about existing [United States sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_sanctions) such as the [Countering America's Adversaries Through Sanctions Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countering_America's_Adversaries_Through_Sanctions_Act) (CAATSA) and possible future sanctions in response to the ongoing [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md). Negotiations are also ongoing to procure limited units which was paid by the government to [Rosoboronexport](3094695.md).[^cite_note-Marcos_Turns_to_US_To_Fill_Canceled_Russian_Helicopter_Contract-186][^cite_note-Marcos_committed_to_continuing_AFP_modernization_program_–_military_official-187]

Marcos expressed support for the [AFP Modernization Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFP_Modernization_Act),[^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_eyes_‘stronger,_bigger’_air_force_to_defend_PH-188] which aims to boost the country's defense capabilities. Stating that the country's external security situation is becoming "more complex and unpredictable", Marcos ordered the [Armed Forces of the Philippines](23448.md) to shift its focus on its defense operations against external threats, due to the lower risks in the country's insurgencies, the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), and the potential Chinese invasion of Taiwan.[^cite_note-PBBM_assures_support_for_AFP_modernization,_enhanced_troop_readiness-189][^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos:_AFP_modernization_to_continue_‘to_address_present_dangers’_PH_is_facing-190]

During the 125th-anniversary celebration of the [Philippine Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Navy), Marcos announced plans to acquire the Philippines' first [submarine](28825.md). The French-based [Naval Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Group), along with other contenders, has offered its [Scorpène-class submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorp%C3%A8ne-class_submarine) to strengthen the Navy.[^cite_note-191]

With an aim to enhance the country's defense capabilities, Marcos has approved the "Re-Horizon 3" of the AFP Modernization Program, which is also known as the RAFMP. The \$35 billion plan revised modernization program will be spread out over 10 years and aims to modernize the [Armed Forces of the Philippines](23448.md) based on the Comprehensive Archipelagic Defense Concept (CADC), a defense concept aimed at strengthening the country's external defense deterrence by projecting power within the [Philippine's 200 nautical mile exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_the_Philippines), [Benham Rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benham_Rise), the [Luzon Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon_Strait), and the [Sulu Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Sea) through inter-island defenses doctrines, multi-layered domain strategies, and long-range strike capabilities.[^cite_note-CADC_to_allow_PH_to_defend_sea_lanes_of_communication-192] The concept also aims to strengthen the country's aerial and maritime domain awareness, connectivity, and intelligence capabilities.[^cite_note-Marcos_approved_AFP_modernization's_'Horizon_3'_—Teodoro-193][^cite_note-AFP_modernization's_Horizon_3_to_focus_on_archipelagic_defense-194][^cite_note-Marcos_Jr._approves_military_procurement_‘wish_list’-195]

On April 15, 2025, Marcos signed into law Republic Act No. 12174, prohibiting the development, production, stockpiling, and use of [chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_weapons) in the Philippines.[^cite_note-196]

#### Education

In August 2022, despite the low [COVID-19 vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_Philippines) rate among Filipino students with a total vaccination rate of only 19%, Marcos, along with [Vice President](2271639.md) and [Education Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Education_\(Philippines\)) [Sara Duterte](42504861.md), reopened onsite classes throughout the country, with 46% or 24,000 schools throughout the country reopening their classes on August 22. Meanwhile, 29,721 schools were allowed to continue implementing [blended learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blended_learning) from August to October 2022,[^cite_note-197] while the full implementation of onsite classes began within November 2022, with 97.5% of public schools returning to onsite classes, while the remaining 2.36% of classes were temporarily held online due to the effects of [Severe Tropical Storm Paeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Nalgae).[^cite_note-DepEd:_97.5%_ng_public_schools,_nakabalik_na_sa_full_F2F_classes-198]

Marcos also reviewed the implementation of the [K–12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%E2%80%9312) program as part of his push to modernize the [country's education system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_Philippines), and laid out measures such as system reforms to address the lack of jobs and potential job mismatches, reviewing the usage of English as a medium of instruction in schools, and improving the country's education technology systems.[^cite_note-Marcos:_K-12_program_under_careful_review-199][^cite_note-Marcos_wants_review_of_education_curriculum_to_address_jobs_mismatch-200] Marcos also expressed his support to modernize the country's schools by improving science-related subjects and courses, theoretical aptitude, and vocational skills.[^cite_note-Marcos_vows_upgrade_of_schools,_teachers-201][^cite_note-Bongbong_Marcos_vows_reform_in_PH_education:_Not_in_history_but_in_sciences,_vocational_skills-202]

#### Economy

This section is an excerpt from [Presidency of Bongbong Marcos § Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bongbong_Marcos#Economy).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Presidency_of_Bongbong_Marcos\&action=edit)]

| 2022 | 3rd | 7.6[^cite_note-203] |
| 4th | 7.2[^cite_note-204] |  |
| 2023 | 1st | 6.4[^cite_note-205] |
| 2nd | 4.3[^cite_note-206] |  |
